\left(\sum_{t,x,z,y_0,y_1}w_r(t,x,z,y_0,y_1)\right)^{-1}
\sum_{t,x,z,y_0,y_1}
w_r(t,x,z,y_0,y_1)\,Y(s).
\] % lean: conditionalMean_witness_latentClass

The denominator is
\[
\sum_{t,x,z,y_0,y_1}w_r(t,x,z,y_0,y_1)
=
p_r,
\]
because each Bernoulli factor sums to one over its binary coordinate. % lean: sum_bernoulliMass

For \(s=0\),
\[
\sum_{t,x,z,y_0,y_1}
w_r(t,x,z,y_0,y_1)y_0^{\mathbb R}
=
p_r\sum_{y_0\in\{0,1\}}\beta_{1/4}(y_0)y_0^{\mathbb R}
=
p_r/4,
\]
so
\[
\mu_{0r}(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})=1/4.
\] % lean: witness_latentEffect

For \(s=1\),
\[
\sum_{t,x,z,y_0,y_1}
w_r(t,x,z,y_0,y_1)y_1^{\mathbb R}
=
p_r\sum_{y_1\in\{0,1\}}\beta_{q_r}(y_1)y_1^{\mathbb R}
=
p_r q_r,
\]
and hence
\[
\mu_{1r}(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})=q_r.
\] % lean: witness_latentEffect

Using the definition of the latent-class treatment effect in \cref{obj:def:latent-effect},
\[
\tau_r(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})
=
\mu_{1r}(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})
-
\mu_{0r}(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})
=
q_r-\frac14.
\] % lean: latentEffect

Substituting the two values of \(q_r\) gives
\[
\tau_1(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})
=
\left(\frac12-\varepsilon\right)-\frac14
=
\frac14-\varepsilon,
\qquad
\tau_2(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})
=
\left(\frac12+\varepsilon\right)-\frac14
=
\frac14+\varepsilon.
\] % lean: witness_latentEffect
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:witness-observed-arm-mass}[Observed arm mass]
For any collision-witness effect displacement \(\varepsilon\) and any treatment arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\), suppose that
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Lower bound.)} \(0\le \varepsilon\).
\item \textbf{(Upper bound.)} \(\varepsilon\le 1/8\).
\end{itemize}
For the two-class Bernoulli proxy witness law \(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}\) from \cref{obj:def:two-class-witness}, its observed-data marginal from \cref{obj:def:observed-margin} satisfies
\[
(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})_O(T=t)
=
\begin{cases}
12/25, & t=0,\\
13/25, & t=1.
\end{cases}
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:witness-observed-arm-mass}]
Fix \(\varepsilon\) with \(0\le\varepsilon\le 1/8\) and fix \(t\in\{0,1\}\).

\begin{enumerate}
\item Define the observed arm set
\[
\mathsf{Arm}_t:=\{(T,X,Z,Y)\in O_{2,2}:T=t\}.
\]
For a full-data record \(w\), let \(O(w)=(T(w),X(w),Z(w),Y(w))\).  Then
\[
O^{-1}(\mathsf{Arm}_t)=\{w:T(w)=t\}.
\]
The coordinate map \(O\) is measurable and \(\mathsf{Arm}_t\) is measurable, so \(\{w:T(w)=t\}\) is measurable.  By \cref{obj:def:observed-margin},
\[
(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})_O(\mathsf{Arm}_t)
=
P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}\{w:T(w)=t\}.
\]
% lean: measurableSet_obsArm, obsMap_measurable, obs_witness_arm_mass

\item For \(q\in\mathbb R\) and \(r\in\{0,1\}\), put
\[
\beta(q,r):=
\begin{cases}
q, & r=1,\\
1-q, & r=0.
\end{cases}
\]
The finite witness law in \cref{obj:def:two-class-witness} assigns to the atom indexed by
\[
u\in\{1,2\},
\qquad
s,x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}
\]
the real weight
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak w_{\varepsilon}(u,s,x,z,y_0,y_1)
&=
\mathfrak p_u\,
\beta(\mathfrak p_u,s)\,
\beta(\mathfrak x_u,x)\,
\beta(\mathfrak z_{s,u},z)\,
\beta(1/4,y_0)\,
\beta(\mathfrak y_{u,\varepsilon},y_1),
\end{aligned}
\]
where
\[
\mathfrak p_1=\frac25,\quad
\mathfrak p_2=\frac35,\quad
\mathfrak x_1=\frac15,\quad
\mathfrak x_2=\frac45,
\]
\[
\mathfrak z_{1,1}=\frac7{20},\quad
\mathfrak z_{1,2}=\frac34,\quad
\mathfrak z_{0,1}=\frac3{10},\quad
\mathfrak z_{0,2}=\frac7{10},
\]
and
\[
\mathfrak y_{1,\varepsilon}=\frac12-\varepsilon,\qquad
\mathfrak y_{2,\varepsilon}=\frac12+\varepsilon .
\]
The bounds on \(\varepsilon\) give
\[
0\le \mathfrak y_{u,\varepsilon}\le 1\qquad (u=1,2),
\]
and all the other displayed Bernoulli parameters also lie in \([0,1]\).  Hence every
\(\mathfrak w_{\varepsilon}(u,s,x,z,y_0,y_1)\) is nonnegative.  Therefore the real mass of the full-data arm event is the ordinary finite sum
\[
P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}\{w:T(w)=t\}
=
\sum_{u=1}^{2}
\sum_{s,x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}}
\mathfrak w_{\varepsilon}(u,s,x,z,y_0,y_1)\,\mathbf 1\{s=t\}.
\]
% lean: witnessLaw_real, witnessWeight_nonneg

\item Selecting the treatment coordinate in the finite sum gives
\[
\sum_{u=1}^{2}
\sum_{s,x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}}
\mathfrak w_{\varepsilon}(u,s,x,z,y_0,y_1)\,\mathbf 1\{s=t\}
=
\sum_{u=1}^{2}
\sum_{x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}}
\mathfrak w_{\varepsilon}(u,t,x,z,y_0,y_1).
\]
This is the two-case calculation \(t=0\) and \(t=1\): exactly one value of \(s\) has indicator one.
% lean: obs_witness_arm_mass

\item For each fixed \(u\in\{1,2\}\) and \(t\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\sum_{x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}}
\mathfrak w_{\varepsilon}(u,t,x,z,y_0,y_1)\\
&\quad =
\mathfrak p_u\beta(\mathfrak p_u,t)
\left(\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}}\beta(\mathfrak x_u,x)\right)
\left(\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}}\beta(\mathfrak z_{t,u},z)\right)
\left(\sum_{y_0\in\{0,1\}}\beta(1/4,y_0)\right)
\left(\sum_{y_1\in\{0,1\}}\beta(\mathfrak y_{u,\varepsilon},y_1)\right).
\end{aligned}
\]
Since \(\beta(q,0)+\beta(q,1)=1\) for every \(q\), the four nuisance factors sum to one, and hence
\[
\sum_{x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}}
\mathfrak w_{\varepsilon}(u,t,x,z,y_0,y_1)
=
\mathfrak p_u\beta(\mathfrak p_u,t).
\]
% lean: witnessWeight_sum_nuisance

\item Combining the preceding identities,
\[
(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})_O(T=t)
=
\sum_{u=1}^{2}\mathfrak p_u\beta(\mathfrak p_u,t).
\]
If \(t=0\), then
\[
\sum_{u=1}^{2}\mathfrak p_u\beta(\mathfrak p_u,0)
=
\frac25\frac35+\frac35\frac25
=
\frac{12}{25}.
\]
If \(t=1\), then
\[
\sum_{u=1}^{2}\mathfrak p_u\beta(\mathfrak p_u,1)
=
\frac25\frac25+\frac35\frac35
=
\frac{13}{25}.
\]
Thus
\[
(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})_O(T=t)
=
\begin{cases}
12/25, & t=0,\\
13/25, & t=1.
\end{cases}
\]
% lean: obs_witness_arm_mass
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:witness-target-feature-determinant}[Witness feature determinant]
For every collision-witness effect displacement \(\varepsilon\in\mathbb R\) satisfying \(0\le \varepsilon\le 1/8\), let \(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}\) be the two-class Bernoulli proxy witness law from \cref{obj:def:two-class-witness}, and let \(B\) be its target-proxy feature matrix from \cref{obj:def:target-feature}. Then
\[
B_{00}B_{11}-B_{01}B_{10}=\frac35 .
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:witness-target-feature-determinant}]
Fix \(\varepsilon\in\mathbb R\) with \(0\le \varepsilon\le 1/8\).  By \cref{obj:def:target-feature,obj:def:two-class-witness}, the entries of the target-proxy feature matrix are
\[
B_{iu}
=
E_{P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}}\!\left[X_i\mid U=u\right].
\]
Since \(X=(1,X_2)^\top\), with conditional means \(E[X_2\mid U=1]=1/5\) and \(E[X_2\mid U=2]=4/5\), this gives, in the statement's zero-based coordinate order,
\[
B
=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&1\\[0.2em]
1/5&4/5
\end{pmatrix}.
\] % lean: witness_targetFeature
Therefore
\[
B_{00}B_{11}-B_{01}B_{10}
=
1\cdot \frac45-1\cdot \frac15
=
\frac35 .
\] % lean: witness_det_targetFeature
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:witness-reference-feature-determinant}[Witness reference-feature determinant]
For the two-class Bernoulli witness law \(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}\) and the reference-proxy feature matrix \(A_t\) in \cref{obj:def:two-class-witness,obj:def:reference-feature}, suppose
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Perturbation range.)} The collision-witness effect displacement \(\varepsilon\) satisfies \(0\le \varepsilon\le 1/8\).
\item \textbf{(Arm.)} The treatment arm \(t\) is binary, \(t\in\{0,1\}\).
\end{itemize}
Then
\[
\det(A_t)=\frac25 .
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:witness-reference-feature-determinant}]
By \cref{obj:def:two-class-witness,obj:def:reference-feature}, the entry \(A_t(r,u)\) is the conditional mean of the \(r\)th coordinate of \(Z=(1,Z_2)^\top\) in latent class \(u\in\{1,2\}\) and arm \(t\). The first coordinate of \(Z\) is identically one. Conditional on \((U,T)\), the variable \(Z_2\) is Bernoulli with the displayed conditional means, while the remaining Bernoulli coordinates are nuisance factors whose conditional masses sum to one. Thus
\[
A_0
=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&1\\[0.2em]
3/10&7/10
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
A_1
=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&1\\[0.2em]
7/20&3/4
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
A_t(1,u)=1,\qquad
A_0(2,1)=\frac{3}{10},\quad
A_0(2,2)=\frac{7}{10},\quad
A_1(2,1)=\frac{7}{20},\quad
A_1(2,2)=\frac34 .
\]
The assumptions \(0\le\varepsilon\le1/8\) ensure that these conditioning cells have positive mass, so the displayed conditional means are the ordinary finite conditional averages. % lean: witness_referenceFeature

For a \(2\times2\) matrix \(C\), write
\[
\det(C)=C_{11}C_{22}-C_{12}C_{21}.
\]
There are two arm cases. If \(t=0\), then
\[
\det(A_0)
=
1\cdot\frac{7}{10}-1\cdot\frac{3}{10}
=
\frac25 .
\]
If \(t=1\), then
\[
\det(A_1)
=
1\cdot\frac34-1\cdot\frac{7}{20}
=
\frac{15}{20}-\frac{7}{20}
=
\frac25 .
\]
Since the arm assumption is exactly \(t\in\{0,1\}\), these two computations give \(\det(A_t)=2/5\). % lean: witness_det_referenceFeature
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:path-proxy-rank-margin}[Path proxy-rank margin]
Let \(P_{g,h}\) be the factorization-preserving two-class path law of \cref{obj:def:path-law-family}. Suppose that
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Gap range.)} \(0\le g\le 1/4\).
\item \textbf{(Tangent amplitude.)} \(|h|\le 1/100\).
\end{itemize}
Then \(P_{g,h}\) satisfies the proxy-rank margin in \cref{obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin} with proxy-rank singular-value margin \(\sigma_0=1/10\): the reference-proxy feature matrices \(A_0\) and \(A_1\) from \cref{obj:def:reference-feature}, and the target-proxy feature matrix \(B\) from \cref{obj:def:target-feature}, all formed from \(P_{g,h}\), have smallest singular value at least \(1/10\).
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:path-proxy-rank-margin}]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \Cref{obj:synth_14,obj:def:reference-feature,obj:def:target-feature} give the feature matrices of \(P_{g,h}\). Under \(|h|\le 1/100\), the denominators below are positive, and
\[
A_0(h)=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&1\\[0.2em]
\dfrac{35h+9}{10(5h+3)}&\dfrac{7}{10}
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
A_1(h)=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&1\\[0.2em]
\dfrac{225h+28}{20(15h+4)}&\dfrac{3}{4}
\end{pmatrix},
\]
while
\[
B(h)=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&1\\[0.2em]
\dfrac15&\dfrac{12/25-h/5}{3/5-h}
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Thus the matrices \(A_0,A_1,B\) in \cref{obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin}, formed from \(P_{g,h}\), are \(A_0(h),A_1(h),B(h)\), respectively. % lean: path_referenceFeature, path_targetFeature

\item The undisplaced reference features have singular-value slack \(1/5\). At \(h=0\),
\[
A_0(0)=
\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\[0.2em]3/10&7/10\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
A_1(0)=
\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\[0.2em]7/20&3/4\end{pmatrix}.
\]
For every \(\upsilon=(\upsilon_1,\upsilon_2)^\top\in\mathbb R^2\),
\[
100\left(\|A_0(0)\upsilon\|_2^2-\frac1{25}\|\upsilon\|_2^2\right)
=
105\upsilon_1^2+242\upsilon_1\upsilon_2+145\upsilon_2^2
=
\frac{146(3\upsilon_1-7\upsilon_2)^2+(549\upsilon_1+641\upsilon_2)^2}{2883}\ge0,
\]
and
\[
400\left(\|A_1(0)\upsilon\|_2^2-\frac1{25}\|\upsilon\|_2^2\right)
=
433\upsilon_1^2+1010\upsilon_1\upsilon_2+609\upsilon_2^2
=
\frac{2168(7\upsilon_1-15\upsilon_2)^2+(5015\upsilon_1+5919\upsilon_2)^2}{58329}\ge0.
\]
The variational characterization of the least singular value therefore gives
\[
\sigma_{\min}(A_0(0))\ge\frac15,
\qquad
\sigma_{\min}(A_1(0))\ge\frac15 .
\] % lean: baseReferenceFeature_strictRank, signalMinSingular_lower_fin_two

\item Define the reference-feature entry displacements
\[
\chi_0(h):=\frac{35h+9}{10(5h+3)}-\frac3{10},
\qquad
\chi_1(h):=\frac{225h+28}{20(15h+4)}-\frac7{20}.
\]
Then
\[
A_0(h)=A_0(0)+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\ \chi_0(h)&0\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
A_1(h)=A_1(0)+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\ \chi_1(h)&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Since \(|h|\le1/100\),
\[
10(5h+3)\ge \frac{59}{2},
\qquad
20(15h+4)\ge 77,
\]
and the algebraic identities
\[
\chi_0(h)=\frac{20h}{10(5h+3)},
\qquad
\chi_1(h)=\frac{120h}{20(15h+4)}
\]
give
\[
|\chi_0(h)|\le \frac{20/100}{59/2}=\frac{2}{295}\le\frac1{50},
\qquad
|\chi_1(h)|\le \frac{120/100}{77}=\frac{6}{385}\le\frac1{50}.
\]
For any real \(\chi\),
\[
\left\|
\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\ \chi&0\end{pmatrix}
\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le |\chi|,
\]
because its action sends \(\upsilon\) to \((0,\chi\upsilon_1)^\top\). Weyl's singular-value perturbation inequality therefore yields, for \(t=0,1\),
\[
\sigma_{\min}(A_t(h))
\ge
\sigma_{\min}(A_t(0))-\frac1{50}
\ge
\frac15-\frac1{50}
\ge
\frac1{10}.
\] % lean: pathReferenceFeature_entry_displacement, path_lowerLeft_opNorm, singular_value_weyl, pathReferenceFeature_rankMargin

\item The target feature is handled in the same perturbative form, starting from
\[
B(0)=
\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\[0.2em]1/5&4/5\end{pmatrix}.
\]
For every \(\upsilon\in\mathbb R^2\),
\[
25\left(\|B(0)\upsilon\|_2^2-\frac1{25}\|\upsilon\|_2^2\right)
=
25\upsilon_1^2+58\upsilon_1\upsilon_2+40\upsilon_2^2
=
\frac{159(3\upsilon_1-4\upsilon_2)^2+(187\upsilon_1+236\upsilon_2)^2}{1456}\ge0,
\]
so
\[
\sigma_{\min}(B(0))\ge\frac15.
\]
With
\[
\chi_B(h):=\frac{12/25-h/5}{3/5-h}-\frac45,
\]
we have
\[
B(h)=B(0)+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&\chi_B(h)\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
\chi_B(h)=\frac{(3/5)h}{3/5-h}.
\]
The bound \(|h|\le1/100\) gives \(3/5-h\ge59/100\), hence
\[
|\chi_B(h)|
\le
\frac{(3/5)(1/100)}{59/100}
=
\frac{3}{295}
\le
\frac1{50}.
\]
Also
\[
\left\|
\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&\chi\end{pmatrix}
\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le|\chi|
\]
for any real \(\chi\), since its action sends \(\upsilon\) to \((0,\chi\upsilon_2)^\top\). Another application of Weyl's inequality gives
\[
\sigma_{\min}(B(h))
\ge
\sigma_{\min}(B(0))-\frac1{50}
\ge
\frac15-\frac1{50}
\ge
\frac1{10}.
\] % lean: pathTargetFeature_zero_strictRank, signalMinSingular_lower_fin_two, pathTargetFeature_entry_displacement, path_lowerRight_opNorm, singular_value_weyl, pathTargetFeature_rankMargin

\item Combining the three estimates,
\[
\min\{\sigma_{\min}(A_0),\sigma_{\min}(A_1),\sigma_{\min}(B)\}
=
\min\{\sigma_{\min}(A_0(h)),\sigma_{\min}(A_1(h)),\sigma_{\min}(B(h))\}
\ge \frac1{10}.
\]
This is the proxy-rank margin of \cref{obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin} with \(\sigma_0=1/10\). % lean: path_proxyRankMargin
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

These linear-algebra statements justify the rank thresholding and anchored diagonalization used by the lattice estimator. The condition-number bound provides an explicit finite-dimensional constant for perturbing between feature coordinates and the compressed spectral coordinates.

The Wasserstein comparisons use a one-dimensional dual potential and the coordinate relation between the Euclidean summary metric and \(d_S\).

\begin{lemmav}{lem:path-model-membership}[Path model membership]
Let \(g,h\in\mathbb R\). Suppose that
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Gap range.)} \(0\le g\le 1/4\).
\item \textbf{(Path displacement.)} \(|h|\le 1/100\).
\end{itemize}
Then the path law \(P_{g,h}\) of \cref{obj:def:path-law-family} belongs to the uniformly conditioned model class \(\mathcal M\) of \cref{obj:def:model-class} with
\[
k=d_x=d_z=2,\qquad L=2,\qquad \pi_0=1/10,\qquad \sigma_0=1/10 .
\]
Equivalently, \(P_{g,h}\) satisfies the core numerical domain for \(k=d_x=d_z=2\), together with \cref{obj:ass:reference-proxy-separation,obj:ass:target-proxy-separation,obj:ass:consistency,obj:ass:latent-ignorability,obj:ass:anchor,obj:ass:bounded-x,obj:ass:bounded-proxy-product,obj:ass:bounded-outcome-proxy-product,obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity,obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin}, using latent-arm positivity margin \(1/10\) and proxy-rank margin \(1/10\).
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:path-model-membership}]
\begin{enumerate}
\item Write the two latent classes as \(u=1,2\), and write \(t,x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}\) for the binary coordinates in the finite support of the path.  Put
\[
p_1=\frac25+h,\qquad p_2=\frac35-h,
\]
and
\[
\begin{array}{c|cc}
&u=1&u=2\\ \hline
w_1(u)&\frac4{25}+\frac{3h}{5}&\frac9{25}-\frac{3h}{5}\\[0.25em]
w_0(u)&\frac6{25}+\frac{2h}{5}&\frac6{25}-\frac{2h}{5}.
\end{array}
\]
For a Bernoulli coordinate \(r\in\{0,1\}\) and success probability \(\theta\), set
\[
\operatorname{bm}(r;\theta)
=
\begin{cases}
1-\theta,& r=0,\\
\theta,& r=1.
\end{cases}
\]
The path assigns the atom \((u,t,x,z,y_0,y_1)\) mass
\[
p_u\,
\operatorname{bm}\!\left(t;\frac{w_1(u)}{p_u}\right)
\operatorname{bm}(x;\beta_u)
\operatorname{bm}(z;\alpha_{t,u})
\operatorname{bm}\!\left(y_0;\frac14\right)
\operatorname{bm}(y_1;\eta_u),
\]
where
\[
\beta_1=\frac15,\qquad
\beta_2=\frac{12/25-h/5}{3/5-h},
\]
\[
\alpha_{1,1}=\frac{225h+28}{20(15h+4)},\quad
\alpha_{1,2}=\frac34,\quad
\alpha_{0,1}=\frac{35h+9}{10(5h+3)},\quad
\alpha_{0,2}=\frac7{10},
\]
and
\[
\eta_1=\frac12-\frac g2,\qquad \eta_2=\frac12+\frac g2 .
\]
Since \(|h|\le 1/100\),
\[
\frac25+h\ge\frac{39}{100},\qquad
\frac35-h\ge\frac{59}{100},\qquad
15h+4\ge\frac{77}{20},\qquad
5h+3\ge\frac{59}{20}.
\]
A direct check of the two latent classes gives
\[
0\le \frac{w_1(u)}{p_u}\le1,\qquad
0\le \beta_u\le1,\qquad
0\le \alpha_{t,u}\le1,
\]
and \(0\le\eta_u\le1\) follows from \(0\le g\le1/4\).  Thus every displayed atom mass is nonnegative.  Summing first over the Bernoulli success and failure values gives
\[
\sum_{u,t,x,z,y_0,y_1}
p_u\,
\operatorname{bm}\!\left(t;\frac{w_1(u)}{p_u}\right)
\operatorname{bm}(x;\beta_u)
\operatorname{bm}(z;\alpha_{t,u})
\operatorname{bm}\!\left(y_0;\frac14\right)
\operatorname{bm}(y_1;\eta_u)
=
p_1+p_2=1 .
\]
Hence \(P_{g,h}\) is a probability law on the full-data record space with \(k=d_x=d_z=2\). % lean: pathLaw_isProbabilityMeasure

\item The numerical core domain in \cref{obj:def:model-class} is
\[
2\le2,\qquad 2\le2,\qquad 2\le2,\qquad 1\le2,\qquad
0<\frac1{10}\le\frac1{2\cdot2},\qquad
0<\frac1{10}\le1 .
\]
% lean: path_ucvmwModel

\item For \cref{obj:ass:reference-proxy-separation}, fix a latent-arm cell \((U=u,T=t)\).  For bounded measurable test functions \(\varphi_Z\) of \(Z\) and \(\psi_{XY}\) of \((X,Y)\), the finite conditional sums over the atom weights factor as
\[
E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[
\varphi_Z(Z)\psi_{XY}(X,Y)\mid U=u,T=t
\right]
=
E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[
\varphi_Z(Z)\mid U=u,T=t
\right]
E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[
\psi_{XY}(X,Y)\mid U=u,T=t
\right].
\]
Indeed, conditional on \((U=u,T=t)\), the factor depending on \(z\) is
\(\operatorname{bm}(z;\alpha_{t,u})\), while the factors determining \(X\) and \(Y\) are functions of \(x,y_0,y_1\) and the fixed arm \(t\).  The normalizing denominator is the same finite latent-arm mass in all three conditional means, so expanding the four binary nuisance sums gives the displayed product identity. % lean: path_referenceProxySeparation

\item For \cref{obj:ass:target-proxy-separation}, fix \(U=u\).  For bounded measurable test functions \(\varphi_X\) of \(X\) and \(\psi_{YT}\) of \((Y,T)\),
\[
E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[
\varphi_X(X)\psi_{YT}(Y,T)\mid U=u
\right]
=
E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[
\varphi_X(X)\mid U=u
\right]
E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[
\psi_{YT}(Y,T)\mid U=u
\right].
\]
After conditioning on \(U=u\), summing over \(z\) uses
\[
\sum_{z=0}^1\operatorname{bm}(z;\alpha_{t,u})=1
\]
for each arm \(t\).  The remaining finite sum separates the \(x\)-factor \(\operatorname{bm}(x;\beta_u)\) from the factors depending on \(t,y_0,y_1\), and hence on \((Y,T)\). % lean: path_targetProxySeparation

\item For \cref{obj:ass:latent-ignorability}, fix a treatment state \(r\in\{0,1\}\) and a latent class \(u\).  For bounded measurable test functions \(\varphi_r\) of the potential outcome \(Y(r)\) and \(\psi_T\) of \(T\),
\[
E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[
\varphi_r(Y(r))\psi_T(T)\mid U=u
\right]
=
E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[
\varphi_r(Y(r))\mid U=u
\right]
E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[
\psi_T(T)\mid U=u
\right].
\]
This is the same finite-product calculation: the Bernoulli factor for \(Y(r)\) depends on \(u\) and \(r\), while the Bernoulli factor for \(T\) is \(\operatorname{bm}(t;w_1(u)/p_u)\). % lean: path_armwiseLatentIgnorability

\item Every support point of \(P_{g,h}\) records the observed outcome as \(Y=Y(T)\), which gives \cref{obj:ass:consistency}.  Every support point also has \(X=(1,x)^\top\), which gives \(X_1=1\) and hence \cref{obj:ass:anchor}. % lean: path_consistency path_anchor

\item The boundedness conditions hold supportwise.  Since \(X=(1,x)^\top\) and \(Z=(1,z)^\top\) with \(x,z\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\|X\|_2\le\sqrt2\le2.
\]
For every vector \(v\),
\[
\|ZX^\top v\|_2
=
\|Z\|_2\,|X^\top v|
\le
\|Z\|_2\,\|X\|_2\,\|v\|_2
\le
2\|v\|_2,
\]
so \(\|ZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2\).  Since \(Y\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\|YZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le \|ZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2 .
\]
Thus \cref{obj:ass:bounded-x,obj:ass:bounded-proxy-product,obj:ass:bounded-outcome-proxy-product} hold with \(L=2\). % lean: path_boundedX path_boundedProxyProduct path_boundedOutcomeProxyProduct

\item The latent-arm cell masses are precisely the four displayed values \(w_t(u)\).  From \(|h|\le1/100\),
\[
\frac4{25}+\frac{3h}{5}\ge\frac4{25}-\frac3{500}\ge\frac1{10},\qquad
\frac9{25}-\frac{3h}{5}\ge\frac9{25}-\frac3{500}\ge\frac1{10},
\]
and
\[
\frac6{25}+\frac{2h}{5}\ge\frac6{25}-\frac1{250}\ge\frac1{10},\qquad
\frac6{25}-\frac{2h}{5}\ge\frac6{25}-\frac1{250}\ge\frac1{10}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\min_{u\in\{1,2\},\,t\in\{0,1\}}
P_{g,h}(U=u,T=t)
\ge
\frac1{10},
\]
which is \cref{obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity} with \(\pi_0=1/10\). % lean: path_latentCell_mass path_latentArmPositivity

\item By \cref{obj:lem:path-proxy-rank-margin},
\[
\min\{\sigma_2(A_0),\sigma_2(A_1),\sigma_2(B)\}\ge\frac1{10}
\]
for the reference- and target-proxy feature matrices formed from \(P_{g,h}\).  Hence \cref{obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin} holds with \(\sigma_0=1/10\). % lean: path_proxyRankMargin

\item Combining the core domain with the model restrictions just verified gives
\[
P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M
\]
for \(k=d_x=d_z=2\), \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\), as required by \cref{obj:def:model-class}. % lean: path_ucvmwModel
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:path-gap-stratum-membership}[Path stratum membership]
For real numbers \(g\) and \(h\), suppose that
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Gap scale.)} The local effect-gap scale satisfies \(0<g\le 1/4\).
\item \textbf{(Path displacement.)} The path displacement satisfies \(|h|\le 1/100\).
\end{itemize}
Then the path law \(P_{g,h}\) of \cref{obj:def:path-law-family} belongs to the gap-local stratum \(\mathcal M(g)\) of \cref{obj:def:gap-stratum}, with \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\). Equivalently, \(P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M\) as specified by \cref{obj:def:model-class,obj:ass:bounded-x,obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity,obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin}, its smallest positive effect gap \(\delta(P_{g,h})\) from \cref{obj:def:effect-gap} satisfies the gap window of \cref{obj:ass:gap-window},
\[
\frac{g}{2}\le \delta(P_{g,h})\le 2g,
\]
and its latent effects from \cref{obj:def:latent-effect} are distinct as in \cref{obj:ass:distinct-effects}.
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:path-gap-stratum-membership}]
Write \(P=P_{g,h}\).  The hypothesis \(0<g\le 1/4\) gives \(0\le g\).  Also \(|h|\le 1/100\) gives
\[
-\frac1{100}\le h\le \frac1{100},
\qquad\text{hence}\qquad
-1\le h\le 1 .
\]
Thus the detailed path construction in \cref{obj:synth_14} applies throughout the tangent-amplitude domain and yields the full-data probability law \(P_{g,h}\). % lean: pathLaw_isProbabilityMeasure

Under the same bounds \(0\le g\le 1/4\) and \(|h|\le1/100\), \cref{obj:lem:path-model-membership} gives
\[
P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M
\]
for \(k=d_x=d_z=2\), \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\).  This supplies the model restrictions in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, including \cref{obj:ass:bounded-x,obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity,obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin}. % lean: path_ucvmwModel

It remains to check the gap-local conditions.  By the explicit construction in \cref{obj:synth_14}, the latent-class masses are
\[
p_1(P)=\frac25+h,
\qquad
p_2(P)=\frac35-h .
\]
The displacement bound gives
\[
p_1(P)\ge \frac25-\frac1{100}=\frac{39}{100}>0,
\qquad
p_2(P)\ge \frac35-\frac1{100}=\frac{59}{100}>0 .
\]
These are the positive-mass classes entering \cref{obj:def:effect-gap,obj:ass:distinct-effects}. % lean: path_latentMass

Again by \cref{obj:synth_14}, the latent conditional potential-outcome means are
\[
\mu_{01}=\mu_{02}=\frac14,
\qquad
\mu_{11}=\frac12-\frac g2,
\qquad
\mu_{12}=\frac12+\frac g2 .
\]
Using \cref{obj:def:latent-effect}, the corresponding latent effects are
\[
\tau_1(P)=\mu_{11}-\mu_{01}
=\frac14-\frac g2,
\qquad
\tau_2(P)=\mu_{12}-\mu_{02}
=\frac14+\frac g2 .
\]
% lean: path_latentMean
% lean: path_latentEffect

Since \(g>0\), these two effects are distinct, and their distance is
\[
|\tau_1(P)-\tau_2(P)|
=
\left|
\left(\frac14-\frac g2\right)
-
\left(\frac14+\frac g2\right)
\right|
=
|-g|
=
g .
\]
The same value is obtained with the two classes interchanged, while equal-index pairs are excluded by the condition \(\tau_u\ne\tau_v\) in \cref{obj:def:effect-gap}.  Hence
\[
\bigl\{|\tau_u(P)-\tau_v(P)|:
p_u(P)>0,\ p_v(P)>0,\ \tau_u(P)\ne\tau_v(P)\bigr\}
=
\{g\},
\]
and therefore
\[
\delta(P)=g .
\]
% lean: path_effectGap

Consequently,
\[
\frac g2\le \delta(P)\le 2g,
\]
because \(\delta(P)=g\) and \(g>0\).  Also,
\[
\tau_1(P)=\frac14-\frac g2
<
\frac14+\frac g2
=\tau_2(P),
\]
so the positive-mass latent effects take exactly \(2=k\) distinct values.  This is \cref{obj:ass:distinct-effects}. % lean: path_distinctEffects

Combining \(P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M\), the gap domain \(0<g\le1/4\), the displayed gap window, and distinct effects gives
\[
P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M(g)
\]
with \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\), as required by \cref{obj:def:gap-stratum}. % lean: path_gapStratum
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:path-local-weight-experiment}[Path local weight membership]
Let \(n\in\mathbb N\), let \(c_{\mathrm{loc}}\in\mathbb R\) be the local Kullback--Leibler radius constant, and let \(g,h\in\mathbb R\). Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Local radius.)} \(0<c_{\mathrm{loc}}<1\), as in \cref{obj:def:local-kl-radius}.
\item \textbf{(Gap scale.)} The local effect-gap scale \(g\) is in the gap window of \cref{obj:ass:gap-window}, with \(0<g\le 1/4\).
\item \textbf{(Path displacement.)} The path displacement satisfies \(|h|\le 1/100\).
\item \textbf{(Observed KL calibration.)}
\[
16000\,g^2h^2\le \frac{c_{\mathrm{loc}}}{n}.
\]
\end{itemize}
Then the path law \(P_{g,h}\) from \cref{obj:def:path-law-family} belongs to the local labeled-weight experiment \(\mathcal L_{n,g}^p\) of \cref{obj:def:local-weight-experiment} with \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\). Equivalently, \(P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M(g)\) as in \cref{obj:def:gap-stratum}, and its observed-data marginal from \cref{obj:def:observed-margin} obeys the neighborhood condition of \cref{obj:ass:local-weight-neighborhood}:
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left((P_{g,h})_O\,\Vert\,(P_{g,0})_O\right)
\le \frac{c_{\mathrm{loc}}}{n},
\qquad
P_{g,0}=P^{\mathrm{wit}}_{g/2}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:path-local-weight-experiment}]
The bound \(|h|\le 1/100\) gives \(-1/100\le h\le 1/100\), hence also \(h\in[-1,1]\).  On this range the latent masses
\[
\frac25+h,\qquad \frac35-h
\]
belong to \([0,1]\).  The treatment Bernoulli probabilities are obtained by dividing
\[
\frac4{25}+\frac35h,\qquad \frac9{25}-\frac35h
\]
by the corresponding latent masses, and the same bounds on \(h\) make each numerator nonnegative and no larger than its denominator.  The target-proxy probabilities
\[
\frac15,\qquad \frac{12/25-h/5}{3/5-h}
\]
also lie in \([0,1]\).  For the reference-proxy probabilities, \(|h|\le1/100\) gives positive denominators and
\[
0\le
\frac{225h+28}{20(15h+4)}
\le 1,
\qquad
0\le
\frac{35h+9}{10(5h+3)}
\le 1,
\]
while the remaining displayed values \(3/4\) and \(7/10\) already lie in \([0,1]\).  Finally \(1/4\) and \(1/2\pm g/2\) are Bernoulli probabilities because \(0\le g\le 1/4\).  Thus all finite product weights defining \(P_{g,h}\) in \cref{obj:synth_14} are nonnegative, and summing those product weights over
\[
u\in\{1,2\},\qquad t,x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}
\]
leaves the two latent masses, whose sum is one.  Hence \(P_{g,h}\) is a probability law.  The same argument applies at \(h=0\), and \cref{obj:synth_14} gives
\[
P_{g,0}=P^{\mathrm{wit}}_{g/2}.
\] % lean: pathLaw_isProbabilityMeasure pathLaw_zero

By \cref{obj:lem:path-gap-stratum-membership}, the hypotheses \(0<g\le 1/4\) and \(|h|\le 1/100\) imply
\[
P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M(g)
\]
with \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\).  The local-radius hypothesis supplies
\[
0<c_{\mathrm{loc}}<1 .
\] % lean: path_gapStratum path_localWeightExperiment

It remains to verify the local observed-neighborhood inequality.  Let
\[
\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}}:=\{0,1\}^4 .
\]
For \((\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\in\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}}\), define
\[
\operatorname{vis}_{g,h}(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)
=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\sum_{u=1}^{2}\sum_{\bar y_0\in\{0,1\}}
P_{g,h}(U=u,T=1,X_2=\bar x,Z_2=\bar z,Y(0)=\bar y_0,Y(1)=\bar y),
& \bar t=1,\\[0.9em]
\displaystyle
\sum_{u=1}^{2}\sum_{\bar y_1\in\{0,1\}}
P_{g,h}(U=u,T=0,X_2=\bar x,Z_2=\bar z,Y(0)=\bar y,Y(1)=\bar y_1),
& \bar t=0 .
\end{cases}
\]
Let \(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\) be the atomic law on \(\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}}\) with these singleton masses.  The map
\[
\Psi_{\mathrm{vis}}(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)
=
\bigl(\bar t,(1,\bar x)^\top,(1,\bar z)^\top,\bar y\bigr)
\]
pushes \(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\) to the observed margin:
\[
(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h})\circ\Psi_{\mathrm{vis}}^{-1}=(P_{g,h})_O .
\]
Therefore data processing gives
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left((P_{g,h})_O\,\Vert\,(P_{g,0})_O\right)
\le
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\,\Vert\,\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\right).
\] % lean: pathVisibleLaw pathVisibleLaw_singleton pathVisibleLaw_map InformationTheory.klDiv_map_le pathLaw_observed_kl_bound

At \(h=0\), direct expansion of the sixteen visible cells gives
\[
\begin{array}{c|cc}
\bar t=0,\;(\bar x,\bar z) & \bar y=0 & \bar y=1\\ \hline
(0,0) & 279/2500 & 93/2500\\
(0,1) & 171/2500 & 57/2500\\
(1,0) & 171/2500 & 57/2500\\
(1,1) & 279/2500 & 93/2500
\end{array}
\qquad
\begin{array}{c|cc}
\bar t=1,\;(\bar x,\bar z) & \bar y=0 & \bar y=1\\ \hline
(0,0) & (253+163g)/5000 & (253-163g)/5000\\
(0,1) & (247-23g)/5000 & (247+23g)/5000\\
(1,0) & (29-16g)/625 & (29+16g)/625\\
(1,1) & (71-64g)/625 & (71+64g)/625 .
\end{array}
\]
Since \(0\le g\le 1/4\), every entry in these two tables is at least \(1/1000\).  Hence
\[
\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\{(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\}\ge \frac1{1000}
\qquad
\text{for every }(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\in\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}},
\]
and \(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\ll\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\). % lean: pathVisibleMass_base_floor pathVisibleLaw_base_floor pathVisibleLaw_absolutelyContinuous

For each visible cell, the same finite expansion gives the exact displacement
\[
\operatorname{vis}_{g,h}(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)
-
\operatorname{vis}_{g,0}(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)
=
\begin{cases}
(1-2\bar y)\,\beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(\bar x,\bar z)\,g h,
& \bar t=1,\\
0,& \bar t=0,
\end{cases}
\]
where
\[
\beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(0,0)=\frac3{25},\qquad
\beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(0,1)=\frac9{25},\qquad
\beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(1,0)=\frac3{100},\qquad
\beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(1,1)=\frac9{100}.
\]
All four coefficients are at most one, and \(g\ge0\), so
\[
\left|
\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\{(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\}
-
\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\{(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\}
\right|
\le g|h|
\]
for every visible cell. % lean: pathVisibleMass_diff_formula pathVisibleMass_diff_bound pathVisibleLaw_singleton

For finite probability laws \(\mathfrak p\ll\mathfrak q\) with positive base mass on every cell,
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathfrak p\Vert\mathfrak q)
\le
\chi^2(\mathfrak p,\mathfrak q),
\qquad
\chi^2(\mathfrak p,\mathfrak q)
=
\sum_{\upsilon\in\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}}}
\frac{(\mathfrak p\{\upsilon\}-\mathfrak q\{\upsilon\})^2}{\mathfrak q\{\upsilon\}},
\]
using \(\log r\le r-1\) for the first inequality.  Applying this with \(\mathfrak p=\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\) and \(\mathfrak q=\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\), the preceding floor and displacement bound yield, cell by cell,
\[
\frac{
\left(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\{\upsilon\}-\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\{\upsilon\}\right)^2
}{
\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\{\upsilon\}
}
\le
1000\,g^2h^2 .
\]
There are \(16\) visible cells, and therefore
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\,\Vert\,\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\right)
\le
16000\,g^2h^2 .
\] % lean: klDiv_le_chiSqDiv pathVisibleLaw_chiSqDiv_bound

Combining data processing with the last display and the assumed calibration gives
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left((P_{g,h})_O\,\Vert\,(P_{g,0})_O\right)
\le
16000\,g^2h^2
\le
\frac{c_{\mathrm{loc}}}{n}.
\]
Using \(P_{g,0}=P^{\mathrm{wit}}_{g/2}\), this is exactly the neighborhood condition in \cref{obj:ass:local-weight-neighborhood}.  Together with \(P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M(g)\), it proves
\[
P_{g,h}\in\mathcal L_{n,g}^p
\]
as defined in \cref{obj:def:local-weight-experiment}. % lean: pathLaw_observed_kl_bound ENNReal.ofReal_le_ofReal path_localWeightExperiment
\end{proof}

The potential lemma is the dual device behind the \(W_1\) modulus for represented atomic laws. The two summary-metric lemmas place the finite-dimensional summary topology and the selection loss on a common continuous footing.

The next group records the witness and path bookkeeping used in the lower-bound section. The statements give explicit masses, effects, observed arm probabilities, determinant margins, model membership, and local-experiment membership for the two-class laws.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_26}[Ordered aggregate mass map]
For a labelled representative \(\xi=\sum_{i=1}^k w_i\delta_{x_i}\in\mathcal P_{\le k}([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\), define \(\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi)\in\mathbb R^k\) as follows. If every labelled weight is positive and the labelled atoms are distinct, then its \(j\)th coordinate is the mass of the atom whose support rank is \(j\):
\[
\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi)_j
:=\sum_{i=1}^k w_i\mathbf 1\!\left\{\#\{\ell:x_\ell<x_i\}=j\right\},
\qquad j=0,\ldots,k-1.
\]
If some labelled weight is zero or two labelled atoms coincide, set \(\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi):=(1/k,\ldots,1/k)\).
\end{definitionv}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:ordered-masses-measure-invariance}[Ordered masses are representative-invariant]
Fix \(k\in\mathbb N\) and a real support radius.  Let \(\nu\) and \(\xi\) be valid labelled atomic representatives with \(k\) slots in the corresponding compact interval, as in \cref{obj:def:atomic-law-class}.  If \(\nu\) and \(\xi\) represent the same aggregated probability measure, then their ordered-mass vectors coincide:
\[
\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\nu)=\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi).
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:ordered-masses-measure-invariance}]
Write the two labelled representatives as
\[
\nu=\bigl((\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i})_{\mathfrak i\in I_k},
(\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i})_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}\bigr),
\qquad
\xi=\bigl((\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak i})_{\mathfrak i\in I_k},
(\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak i})_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}\bigr),
\qquad
I_k:=\{0,\ldots,k-1\}.
\]
Validity means
\[
0\le \varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i},\quad
0\le \varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak i},\quad
\sum_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}=1,\quad
\sum_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak i}=1,
\]
with all labelled atoms lying in the prescribed compact interval.  For any labelled representative
\(\zeta=((\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}),(\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}))\), put
\[
\operatorname{supp}_+(\zeta)
:=
\{\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}:\mathfrak i\in I_k,\ 0<\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\}.
\]
If \(k=0\), there are no vector coordinates to check.

\begin{enumerate}
\item\label{pf:ordered-masses:aggregate}
The equality of represented probability measures gives equality of aggregate mass at every real point:
\[
\sum_{\mathfrak i:\,\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}=\mathfrak x}\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}
=
\sum_{\mathfrak j:\,\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}=\mathfrak x}\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}
\qquad\text{for every }\mathfrak x\in\mathbb R .
\]
Indeed, by \cref{obj:def:atomic-law-class,obj:synth_28}, the represented measure assigns \(\{\mathfrak x\}\) the aggregate of the positive parts of the labelled weights at \(\mathfrak x\); validity gives \(\max\{\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i},0\}=\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\) and \(\max\{\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j},0\}=\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}\).  Evaluating the common represented measure on \(\{\mathfrak x\}\) gives the displayed identity.
% lean: AtomicLaw.ProbabilityLaw.MeasureEquivalent.aggregate_weight

\item\label{pf:ordered-masses:support}
Consequently,
\[
\operatorname{supp}_+(\nu)=\operatorname{supp}_+(\xi).
\]
If \(\mathfrak x=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\) and \(0<\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\), then the aggregate mass of \(\nu\) at \(\mathfrak x\) is positive.  By \cref{pf:ordered-masses:aggregate}, the aggregate mass of \(\xi\) at \(\mathfrak x\) is positive, and nonnegativity of the labelled weights yields some \(\mathfrak j\in I_k\) with
\[
\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}=\mathfrak x,
\qquad
0<\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}.
\]
This proves one inclusion, and the reverse inclusion follows by interchanging \(\nu\) and \(\xi\).
% lean: support_eq_of_measureEquivalent

\item\label{pf:ordered-masses:rank}
For a representative \(\zeta\), define the rank of a labelled slot by
\[
\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)
:=
\#\{\mathfrak q\in I_k:\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak q}<\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\}.
\]
The counted set excludes \(\mathfrak i\), so
\[
\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)<k .
\]
Moreover, if \(\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}<\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak j}\), then
\[
\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)
<
\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak j),
\]
because the first counted set is contained in the second and \(\mathfrak i\) belongs to the second but not the first.  Hence, whenever
\(\mathfrak i\mapsto\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\) is injective, the map
\(\mathfrak i\mapsto\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)\) is injective, and therefore bijective on the finite set \(I_k\).
% lean: atomicRank_lt; atomicRank_lt_of_atom_lt; atomicRank_injective; rank_bijective

\item\label{pf:ordered-masses:full-support}
The following full-support criterion holds:
\[
\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\zeta)=k
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\left[
(\forall \mathfrak i\in I_k,\ 0<\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i})
\ \text{and}\
\mathfrak i\mapsto\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\ \text{is injective}
\right].
\]
For the forward implication, \(\operatorname{supp}_+(\zeta)\) is the image of
\(\{\mathfrak i\in I_k:0<\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\}\) under
\(\mathfrak i\mapsto\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\), so
\[
\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\zeta)
\le
\#\{\mathfrak i\in I_k:0<\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\}
\le k .
\]
If the left side equals \(k\), both inequalities are equalities; thus every labelled weight is positive, and equality in the image-cardinality bound gives injectivity of the labelled locations.  The reverse implication follows because all \(k\) labelled slots are then positive and distinct.
% lean: full_support_iff

\item\label{pf:ordered-masses:apply-rank}
Suppose first that
\[
\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\nu)=k .
\]
By \cref{pf:ordered-masses:support},
\[
\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\xi)=k .
\]
Then \cref{pf:ordered-masses:full-support} gives
\[
0<\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i},
\quad
0<\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak i}
\qquad(\mathfrak i\in I_k),
\]
and both labelled-location maps are injective.  For either such full-support representative \(\zeta\), \cref{obj:synth_26} gives
\[
\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\zeta)_{\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)}
=
\sum_{\mathfrak q:\,
\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak q)=\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)}
\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak q}
=
\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i},
\]
where the last equality uses the rank injectivity from \cref{pf:ordered-masses:rank}.
% lean: orderedMasses_apply_rank

\item\label{pf:ordered-masses:weight-match}
Fix a coordinate \(\mathfrak r\in I_k\).  Since the rank map for \(\nu\) is bijective by \cref{pf:ordered-masses:rank}, choose \(\mathfrak i\in I_k\) such that
\[
\mathfrak r=\operatorname{rank}_{\nu}(\mathfrak i).
\]
The support equality in \cref{pf:ordered-masses:support} gives \(\mathfrak j\in I_k\) with
\[
\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i},
\qquad
0<\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}.
\]
Using \cref{pf:ordered-masses:aggregate} at \(\mathfrak x=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\), and using injectivity of both labelled-location maps to collapse the two sums, yields
\[
\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}
=
\sum_{\mathfrak q:\,\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}}
\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak q}
=
\sum_{\mathfrak q:\,\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak q}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}}
\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak q}
=
\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}.
\]
% lean: orderedMasses_eq_of_measureEquivalent

\item\label{pf:ordered-masses:rank-match}
For each \(\mathfrak q\in I_k\), \cref{pf:ordered-masses:support} supplies a slot
\(\psi(\mathfrak q)\in I_k\) such that
\[
\chi^\xi_{\psi(\mathfrak q)}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q}.
\]
The map \(\psi\) is injective: if \(\psi(\mathfrak q_1)=\psi(\mathfrak q_2)\), then
\(\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q_1}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q_2}\), and injectivity of the locations of \(\nu\) gives
\(\mathfrak q_1=\mathfrak q_2\).  Thus \(\psi\) is bijective.  Since also
\(\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\), for every \(\mathfrak q\in I_k\),
\[
\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q}<\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\chi^\xi_{\psi(\mathfrak q)}<\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}.
\]
The bijection \(\psi\) therefore identifies the two sets counted by the ranks, so
\[
\operatorname{rank}_{\nu}(\mathfrak i)
=
\operatorname{rank}_{\xi}(\mathfrak j).
\]
Combining this rank equality with \cref{pf:ordered-masses:apply-rank,pf:ordered-masses:weight-match} gives
\[
\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\nu)_{\mathfrak r}
=
\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}
=
\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}
=
\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi)_{\mathfrak r}.
\]
Since \(\mathfrak r\) was arbitrary, the ordered-mass vectors are equal in the full-support case.
% lean: orderedMasses_eq_of_measureEquivalent

\item\label{pf:ordered-masses:fallback}
It remains to consider
\[
\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\nu)\ne k .
\]
By \cref{pf:ordered-masses:support},
\[
\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\xi)\ne k .
\]
Using \cref{pf:ordered-masses:full-support}, the positivity-and-injectivity condition in \cref{obj:synth_26} fails for both representatives.  Hence both ordered-mass vectors take the barycentric fallback value:
\[
\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\nu)_{\mathfrak r}
=
k^{-1}
=
\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi)_{\mathfrak r}
\qquad\text{for every }\mathfrak r\in I_k .
\]
Thus the ordered-mass vectors are equal in the complementary case as well.
% lean: orderedMasses_eq_of_measureEquivalent
\end{enumerate}

The two cases prove
\[
\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\nu)=\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi),
\]
as required.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.orderedMasses_eq_of_measureEquivalent
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:observed-outcome-proxy-moment-factorization}[Outcome proxy moment factorization]
Let \(P\), a full-data probability law carrying \((U,T,X,Z,Y(0),Y(1),Y)\), belong to the model class \(\mathcal M\) in \cref{obj:def:model-class}. Suppose that
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Latent classes.)} \(2\le k\).
\item \textbf{(Positivity margin.)} The latent-arm positivity margin satisfies \(\pi_0>0\).
\item \textbf{(Model conditions.)} The reference- and target-proxy separation, consistency, latent ignorability, latent-arm positivity, bounded outcome-proxy product, and bounded target-proxy conditions in \cref{obj:ass:reference-proxy-separation,obj:ass:target-proxy-separation,obj:ass:consistency,obj:ass:latent-ignorability,obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity,obj:ass:bounded-outcome-proxy-product,obj:ass:bounded-x} hold.
\end{itemize}
Then, for each treatment arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
N_t(P)
=
A_t\,\Gamma_t\,\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}:1\le u\le k\}\,B^\top,
\qquad
\Gamma_t
=
\operatorname{diag}\left\{\frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}:1\le u\le k\right\}.
\]
Here \(N_t(P)\) is the observable armwise \(YZX^\top\) population moment in \cref{obj:def:observable-population-moments}, \(A_t\) is the reference-proxy feature matrix in \cref{obj:def:reference-feature}, \(B\) is the target-proxy feature matrix in \cref{obj:def:target-feature}, and \(\mu_{tu}\) is the latent conditional potential-outcome mean in \cref{obj:def:latent-mean}.
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:observed-outcome-proxy-moment-factorization}]
\emph{Step 1.} Fix \(t\in\{0,1\}\).  For \(1\le u\le k\), put
\[
\mathsf U_u:=\{U=u\},\qquad
\mathsf R_t:=\{T=t\},\qquad
\mathsf H_{u,t}:=\mathsf U_u\cap\mathsf R_t .
\]
For a full-data event \(\mathfrak e\) and a real-valued measurable function \(\varphi\), write
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathfrak e}^{P}[\varphi]
:=
\{P(\mathfrak e)\}^{-1}\int_{\mathfrak e}\varphi\,dP,
\]
with the total convention \(0^{-1}=0\).  The latent-arm positivity assumption gives, for every \(u\),
\[
0<\pi_0\le P(\mathsf H_{u,t})\le P(\mathsf U_u).
\]
Also, \cref{obj:lem:arm-mass-lower-bound} gives
\[
k\pi_0\le P(\mathsf R_t),
\]
and since \(2\le k\) and \(0<\pi_0\),
\[
0<k\pi_0\le P(\mathsf R_t).
\]
Thus all normalizations used below have positive denominators on each latent class, each latent-treatment cell, and the treatment arm.
% lean: latentCell_pos_of_latentArmPositivity
% lean: arm_mass_lower_of_latentArmPositivity

\emph{Step 2.} Let \(P_u^\circ\) be the normalized restriction of \(P\) to \(\mathsf U_u\), equivalently the probability law with density \(P(\mathsf U_u)^{-1}\mathbf 1_{\mathsf U_u}\) with respect to \(P\).  Latent ignorability in \cref{obj:ass:latent-ignorability} gives independence of \(Y(t)\) and \(T\) under \(P_u^\circ\).  Therefore
\[
\int_{\mathsf R_t}Y(t)\,dP_u^\circ
=
P_u^\circ(\mathsf R_t)\int Y(t)\,dP_u^\circ,
\qquad
P_u^\circ(\mathsf R_t)=\frac{P(\mathsf H_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf U_u)} .
\]
Using the normalized-restriction formula on both sides,
\[
\frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)}\int_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}Y(t)\,dP
=
\frac{P(\mathsf H_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf U_u)}
\frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)}\int_{\mathsf U_u}Y(t)\,dP .
\]
The positive denominators from Step 1 allow cancellation, giving
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Y(t)]
=
\mathbb E_{\mathsf U_u}^{P}[Y(t)]
=
\mu_{tu},
\]
where the last equality is \cref{obj:def:latent-mean}.  On \(\mathsf R_t\), consistency in \cref{obj:ass:consistency} gives \(Y=Y(t)\) almost surely, hence
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Y]
=
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Y(t)]
=
\mu_{tu}.
\]
% lean: conditionalMean_potential_latentCell
% lean: conditionalMean_observed_eq_potential

\emph{Step 3.} Fix coordinates \(1\le \iota_z\le d_z\) and \(1\le \iota_x\le d_x\).  Target-proxy separation in \cref{obj:ass:target-proxy-separation}, applied under \(P_u^\circ\), gives independence of \(X\) and \((Y,T)\) conditional on \(U=u\).  Define
\[
\psi_t(y,s):=y\,\mathbf 1\{s=t\},
\qquad (y,s)\in\mathbb R\times\{0,1\}.
\]
The product identity under \(P_u^\circ\) gives
\[
\int X_{\iota_x}\psi_t(Y,T)\,dP_u^\circ
=
\left(\int X_{\iota_x}\,dP_u^\circ\right)
\left(\int \psi_t(Y,T)\,dP_u^\circ\right).
\]
The three terms are
\[
\int X_{\iota_x}\psi_t(Y,T)\,dP_u^\circ
=
\frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)}
\int_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}X_{\iota_x}Y\,dP,
\]
\[
\int X_{\iota_x}\,dP_u^\circ
=
\frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)}
\int_{\mathsf U_u}X_{\iota_x}\,dP,
\qquad
\int \psi_t(Y,T)\,dP_u^\circ
=
\frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)}
\int_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}Y\,dP .
\]
Multiplying by \(P(\mathsf U_u)/P(\mathsf H_{u,t})\) and using Step 1 yields
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[X_{\iota_x}Y]
=
\mathbb E_{\mathsf U_u}^{P}[X_{\iota_x}]\,
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Y].
\]
By \cref{obj:def:target-feature} and Step 2,
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[X_{\iota_x}Y]
=
B(\iota_x,u)\,\mu_{tu}.
\]
% lean: conditionalMean_target_mul_observed

\emph{Step 4.} Let \(P_{u,t}^\circ\) be the normalized restriction of \(P\) to \(\mathsf H_{u,t}\).  Reference-proxy separation in \cref{obj:ass:reference-proxy-separation}, applied under \(P_{u,t}^\circ\), gives independence of \(Z\) and \((X,Y)\) on the latent-treatment cell.  Applying the product identity to \(Z_{\iota_z}\) and \(X_{\iota_x}Y\),
\[
\int Z_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}Y\,dP_{u,t}^\circ
=
\left(\int Z_{\iota_z}\,dP_{u,t}^\circ\right)
\left(\int X_{\iota_x}Y\,dP_{u,t}^\circ\right).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Z_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}Y]
=
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Z_{\iota_z}]\,
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[X_{\iota_x}Y].
\]
Combining this identity with Step 3 and \cref{obj:def:reference-feature} gives the cellwise factorization
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}]
=
A_t(\iota_z,u)\,\mu_{tu}\,B(\iota_x,u).
\]
% lean: conditionalMean_reference_targetOutcome
% lean: latentCell_outcomeProxy_factorization

\emph{Step 5.} Set
\[
\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}:=YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}.
\]
The bounded outcome-proxy product condition in \cref{obj:ass:bounded-outcome-proxy-product} implies
\[
|\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}|
=
|YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}|
\le
\|YZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\le L
\]
almost surely, since every matrix entry is bounded by the operator norm.  Thus \(\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}\) is integrable on every latent-treatment cell.  The treatment arm decomposes as the disjoint finite union
\[
\mathsf R_t=\bigsqcup_{u=1}^{k}\mathsf H_{u,t},
\]
so
\[
\int_{\mathsf R_t}\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}\,dP
=
\sum_{u=1}^{k}\int_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}\,dP .
\]
Dividing by the positive arm mass from Step 1 and rewriting each cell integral as a conditional mean gives
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathsf R_t}^{P}[\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}]
=
\sum_{u=1}^{k}
\frac{P(\mathsf H_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf R_t)}
\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}].
\]
Substituting Step 4,
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathsf R_t}^{P}[YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}]
=
\sum_{u=1}^{k}
A_t(\iota_z,u)\,
\frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}\,
\mu_{tu}\,
B(\iota_x,u).
\]
% lean: conditionalMean_fullDataArm_eq_sum_latentCell

\emph{Step 6.} By \cref{obj:def:observed-margin}, \(P_O\) is the pushforward of \(P\) by \(O=(T,X,Z,Y)\).  Hence the observed-arm conditional mean agrees with the corresponding full-data arm conditional mean:
\[
E_{P_O}\!\left[YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}\mid T=t\right]
=
\mathbb E_{\mathsf R_t}^{P}[YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}].
\]
The armwise \(YZX^\top\) moment notation in \cref{obj:def:observable-population-moments} identifies the \((\iota_z,\iota_x)\) entry of \(N_t(P)\) with this same full-data conditional moment.  Therefore
\[
N_t(P)(\iota_z,\iota_x)
=
\sum_{u=1}^{k}
A_t(\iota_z,u)\,
\frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}\,
\mu_{tu}\,
B(\iota_x,u).
\]
With
\[
\Gamma_t
=
\operatorname{diag}\left\{
\frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}:1\le u\le k
\right\},
\]
the \((\iota_z,\iota_x)\) entry of
\[
A_t\,\Gamma_t\,\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}:1\le u\le k\}\,B^\top
\]
is exactly the displayed sum.  Since \(\iota_z\) and \(\iota_x\) were arbitrary,
\[
N_t(P)
=
A_t\,\Gamma_t\,\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}:1\le u\le k\}\,B^\top .
\]
% lean: conditionalMean_obsArm_eq_fullDataArm
% lean: observedOutcomeProxyMoment_factorization
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:latent-arm-weight-invertibility}[Latent-arm weight injectivity]
Let \(P\) be a full-data probability law carrying \((U,T,X,Z,Y(0),Y(1),Y)\), and let \(\pi_0>0\). Suppose \(P\) satisfies latent-arm positivity with margin \(\pi_0\), as in \cref{obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity}. Then, for each treatment arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\), the diagonal matrix
\[
\Gamma_t
=
\operatorname{diag}\left\{
\frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}
: u\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
\right\}
\]
defines an injective linear map on \(\mathbb R^k\).
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:latent-arm-weight-invertibility}]
Fix \(t\in\{0,1\}\). For a latent coordinate \(u\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\), set
\[
\mathsf{cell}_{u,t}:=\{U=u,\ T=t\},
\qquad
\mathsf{arm}_{t}:=\{T=t\},
\qquad
\gamma_{u,t}:=\frac{P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf{arm}_t)} .
\]
By the definition of \(\Gamma_t\), this matrix is diagonal and its \(u\)th diagonal entry is \(\gamma_{u,t}\). % lean: latentArmWeights

We first show that every diagonal entry is bounded below by the positivity margin. By \cref{obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity},
\[
\pi_0\le P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t}).
\]
Moreover \(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t}\subseteq \mathsf{arm}_t\), so monotonicity gives
\[
P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t})\le P(\mathsf{arm}_t).
\]
Together with \(\pi_0>0\), this implies
\[
0<P(\mathsf{arm}_t).
\]
Since \(P\) is a probability law,
\[
P(\mathsf{arm}_t)\le 1.
\]
Hence, using \(\pi_0>0\),
\[
\pi_0 P(\mathsf{arm}_t)
\le \pi_0
\le P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t}).
\]
Dividing by the positive number \(P(\mathsf{arm}_t)\) yields
\[
\pi_0
\le
\frac{P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf{arm}_t)}
=
\gamma_{u,t}.
\]
In particular \(\gamma_{u,t}>0\), so \(\gamma_{u,t}\ne0\). % lean: latentArmWeight_lower

Now let \(x,y\in\mathbb R^k\) and suppose \(\Gamma_t x=\Gamma_t y\). Taking the \(u\)th coordinate and using diagonality gives
\[
\gamma_{u,t}x_u=\gamma_{u,t}y_u .
\]
Since \(\gamma_{u,t}\ne0\), cancellation gives
\[
x_u=y_u .
\]
This holds for every latent coordinate \(u\), and therefore \(x=y\). Thus the linear map induced by \(\Gamma_t\) is injective. % lean: latentArmWeights_injective
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:moore-penrose-product-cancellation}[Moore--Penrose product cancellation]
Let \(C\in\mathbb R^{\mathrm{rows}\times r}\), \(B\in\mathbb R^{\mathrm{cols}\times r}\), and \(\mu\in\mathbb R^r\). Suppose that the linear maps represented by \(C\) and by \(B\), each with domain \(\mathbb R^r\), are injective. Then
\[
(CB^\top)^\dagger\bigl(C\operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top\bigr)
=
(B^\dagger)^\top \operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top .
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:moore-penrose-product-cancellation}]
For any real rectangular matrix \(A\), let \(A^\dagger\) denote its Moore--Penrose inverse, characterized by
\[
AA^\dagger A=A,\qquad
A^\dagger A A^\dagger=A^\dagger,\qquad
(AA^\dagger)^\top=AA^\dagger,\qquad
(A^\dagger A)^\top=A^\dagger A .
\]
If the matrix map \(A\) is injective, then
\[
A^\dagger A=I .
\]
Indeed, the first Moore--Penrose identity gives
\[
A(A^\dagger A)=AA^\dagger A=A.
\]
Thus, for every vector \(x\),
\[
A\{(A^\dagger A)x\}=Ax,
\]
and injectivity of \(A\) gives \((A^\dagger A)x=x\). Hence \(A^\dagger A=I\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.AmbientOperatorBridge.moorePenroseInverse_mul_eq_one_of_injective

Apply this observation to \(C\) and \(B\). Then
\[
C^\dagger C=I_r,\qquad B^\dagger B=I_r,
\]
and transposing the second identity gives
\[
B^\top (B^\dagger)^\top=I_r . % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.AmbientOperatorBridge.moorePenroseInverse_mul_eq_one_of_injective
\]
Set
\[
G:=(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger .
\]
We verify that \(G\) is the Moore--Penrose inverse of \(CB^\top\). First,
\[
(CB^\top)G(CB^\top)
=
CB^\top (B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger C B^\top
=
C I_r I_r B^\top
=
CB^\top ,
\]
and
\[
G(CB^\top)G
=
(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger C B^\top (B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger
=
(B^\dagger)^\top I_r I_r C^\dagger
=
G . % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.AmbientOperatorBridge.moorePenroseInverse_mul_transpose
\]
For the symmetry equations, the same cancellations give
\[
(CB^\top)G=CB^\top(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger=CC^\dagger,
\]
so \(((CB^\top)G)^\top=(CB^\top)G\) by the Moore--Penrose symmetry of \(CC^\dagger\). Also
\[
G(CB^\top)=(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger C B^\top=(B^\dagger)^\top B^\top=(BB^\dagger)^\top=BB^\dagger,
\]
so \((G(CB^\top))^\top=G(CB^\top)\) by the Moore--Penrose symmetry of \(BB^\dagger\). Thus \(G\) satisfies all four Moore--Penrose equations for \(CB^\top\), and uniqueness yields
\[
(CB^\top)^\dagger=(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger . % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.AmbientOperatorBridge.moorePenroseInverse_mul_transpose
\]

Therefore, using associativity and \(C^\dagger C=I_r\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
(CB^\top)^\dagger\bigl(C\operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top\bigr)
&=(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger C\operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top \\
&=(B^\dagger)^\top \operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top .
\end{aligned}
\]
This is the claimed cancellation identity. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.AmbientOperatorBridge.moorePenrose_outcome_factorization
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:kantorovich-rubinstein-attaining-potential}[Attaining KR potential]
Let \(\nu\) and \(\xi\) be valid represented atomic laws with finite labelled slot sets: their weights are nonnegative and sum to one.  Let \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}\) be the cumulative-distribution-gap sign Kantorovich--Rubinstein potential selected for the pair \((\nu,\xi)\).  Then \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}\) is one-Lipschitz, is normalized by
\[
\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(0)=0,
\]
and attains the Wasserstein distance of \cref{obj:def:wasserstein-distance}:
\[
W_1(\nu,\xi)
=
\left|
  \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu
  -
  \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi
\right|.
\]
Moreover, for every \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\), if every one-Lipschitz function \(\varphi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) satisfying \(\varphi(0)=0\) obeys
\[
\left|
  \int \varphi\,d\nu
  -
  \int \varphi\,d\xi
\right|
\le \epsilon,
\]
then
\[
W_1(\nu,\xi)\le \epsilon.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:kantorovich-rubinstein-attaining-potential}]
Write
\[
\nu=\sum_i p_i\delta_{x_i},
\qquad
\xi=\sum_j q_j\delta_{y_j},
\]
with \(p_i,q_j\ge0\), \(\sum_i p_i=\sum_jq_j=1\).  Define
\[
F_\nu(t):=\sum_i p_i{\bf 1}\{x_i\le t\},
\qquad
F_\xi(t):=\sum_j q_j{\bf 1}\{y_j\le t\},
\qquad
\Delta(t):=F_\nu(t)-F_\xi(t),
\]
and let
\[
s(t):=
\begin{cases}
1,&\Delta(t)>0,\\
-1,&\Delta(t)<0,\\
0,&\Delta(t)=0.
\end{cases}
\]
The selected potential is
\[
\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(x):=\int_0^x s(t)\,dt .
\]
Since \(|s(t)|\le1\), for all \(x,y\in\mathbb R\),
\[
|\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(x)-\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(y)|
=
\left|\int_y^x s(t)\,dt\right|
\le |x-y|,
\]
so \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}\) is one-Lipschitz.  Also
\[
\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(0)=\int_0^0 s(t)\,dt=0 .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Substrate.CollisionSafeSpectralLaw.AtomicLaw.krPotential_zero

We next record the finite one-dimensional transport identity used by the potential.  For any finite transport plan \(\gamma=(\gamma_{ij})\) from \(\nu\) to \(\xi\), put
\[
C_\gamma(t)
:=
\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}
\bigl(
{\bf 1}\{x_i\le t<y_j\}
-
{\bf 1}\{y_j\le t<x_i\}
\bigr).
\]
Using the two marginal constraints of the plan gives
\[
\Delta(t)=C_\gamma(t)
\qquad\text{for every }t\in\mathbb R .
\]
Hence, with
\[
E_\gamma(t):=
\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}
{\bf 1}\{\min(x_i,y_j)\le t<\max(x_i,y_j)\},
\]
we have
\[
|\Delta(t)|\le E_\gamma(t).
\]
Integrating and using
\[
\int_{\mathbb R}{\bf 1}\{\min(x_i,y_j)\le t<\max(x_i,y_j)\}\,dt
=
|x_i-y_j|
\]
yields
\[
\int_{\mathbb R}|\Delta(t)|\,dt
\le
\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}|x_i-y_j|.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Substrate.CollisionSafeSpectralLaw.AtomicLaw.integral_abs_cdfGap_le_transportCost

Conversely, the finite transport polytope is compact and the quadratic cost
\[
\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}(x_i-y_j)^2
\]
therefore has a minimizer.  If such a minimizer sent positive mass across some cut in both directions, shifting
\(\varepsilon=\min\{\gamma_{ij},\gamma_{kl}\}\) from the crossing pairs \((i,j),(k,l)\) to \((i,l),(k,j)\) would preserve the two marginals and decrease the quadratic cost by
\[
2\varepsilon(x_k-x_i)(y_j-y_l)>0,
\]
a contradiction.  Thus the minimizer has no counterflow across any cut, and for that plan \(|C_\gamma(t)|=E_\gamma(t)\) for every \(t\).  Consequently
\[
\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}|x_i-y_j|
=
\int_{\mathbb R}E_\gamma(t)\,dt
=
\int_{\mathbb R}|\Delta(t)|\,dt .
\]
Together with the preceding inequality and \cref{obj:def:wasserstein-distance}, this gives
\[
W_1(\nu,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R}|\Delta(t)|\,dt .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Substrate.CollisionSafeSpectralLaw.AtomicLaw.w1_eq_integral_abs_cdfGap

It remains to evaluate the potential.  For an atom \(a\), set
\[
\chi_a(t):=
{\bf 1}\{0\le t<a\}-{\bf 1}\{a\le t<0\}.
\]
Then
\[
\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(a)=\int_{\mathbb R}s(t)\chi_a(t)\,dt .
\]
Interchanging the finite sums with the integral gives
\[
\int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu-\int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi
=
\int_{\mathbb R}s(t)
\left\{
\sum_i p_i\chi_{x_i}(t)-\sum_j q_j\chi_{y_j}(t)
\right\}\,dt .
\]
For \(t\ge0\), the expression in braces equals
\((1-F_\nu(t))-(1-F_\xi(t))=-\Delta(t)\); for \(t<0\), it equals
\(-F_\nu(t)+F_\xi(t)=-\Delta(t)\).  Therefore
\[
\int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu-\int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi
=
-\int_{\mathbb R}s(t)\Delta(t)\,dt
=
-\int_{\mathbb R}|\Delta(t)|\,dt .
\]
Taking absolute values and using the preceding display for \(W_1\) gives
\[
W_1(\nu,\xi)
=
\left|
  \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu
  -
  \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi
\right|.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Substrate.CollisionSafeSpectralLaw.AtomicLaw.krPotential_attains

Finally, suppose \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\) and every normalized one-Lipschitz test function satisfies the displayed bound.  Applying that bound to the just-constructed \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}\), which is one-Lipschitz and satisfies \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(0)=0\), gives
\[
W_1(\nu,\xi)
=
\left|
  \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu
  -
  \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi
\right|
\le \epsilon .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.krPotential_attains_with_normalization
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:summary-metric-coordinate-comparison}[Coordinate metric comparison]
Let \(d_x\) and \(d_z\) be fixed target- and reference-proxy dimensions, and let \(s,s'\) be elements of the five-block observable summary space in \cref{obj:def:observable-summary}. Equip this space with its coordinate Euclidean distance \(\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}\). Then
\[
d_S(s,s')
\le
\bigl(4\operatorname{Ent}_{d_z,d_x}+d_x\bigr)\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}(s,s'),
\]
where
\[
\operatorname{Ent}_{r,c}
:=
c\,r\,\|J_c\|_{\mathrm{op}},
\]
and \(J_c:\mathbb R^c\to(\{1,\ldots,c\}\to\mathbb R)\) is the canonical coordinate identification.
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:summary-metric-coordinate-comparison}]
Write
\[
\varrho:=\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}(s,s').
\]
For \(d\in\mathbb N\), put
\[
[d]_0:=\{0,1,\ldots,d-1\};
\]
when \(d=0\), this set is empty and the finite sums below have value zero.  Write
\[
s=(s_1,s_2,s_3,s_4,s_5),
\qquad
s'=(s'_1,s'_2,s'_3,s'_4,s'_5),
\]
where \(s_1,\ldots,s_4\) and \(s'_1,\ldots,s'_4\) are \(d_z\times d_x\) matrix
blocks and \(s_5,s'_5\in\mathbb R^{d_x}\), as in
\cref{obj:def:observable-summary}.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Since \(\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}\) is the Euclidean distance on the full
coordinate vector, each coordinate-block projection is nonexpansive.  Hence,
for each \(\ell=1,2,3,4\),
\[
\left(
  \sum_{\mathfrak i\in[d_z]_0,\ \mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0}
  \bigl((s_\ell)_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}
        -(s'_\ell)_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}\bigr)^2
\right)^{1/2}
\le \varrho,
\]
and
\[
\left(
  \sum_{\mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0}
  \bigl((s_5)_{\mathfrak j}-(s'_5)_{\mathfrak j}\bigr)^2
\right)^{1/2}
\le \varrho .
\]
In particular,
\[
\bigl|(s_\ell)_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}
      -(s'_\ell)_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}\bigr|
\le \varrho
\quad
(\ell=1,2,3,4;\ \mathfrak i\in[d_z]_0,\ \mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0),
\]
and
\[
\bigl|(s_5)_{\mathfrak j}-(s'_5)_{\mathfrak j}\bigr|
\le \varrho
\quad(\mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0).
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.dS_le_dist_mul

\item We use the following finite-dimensional entrywise estimate.  Let
\(\Psi\in\mathbb R^{r\times c}\), let \(\zeta\in\mathbb R\) satisfy
\(0\le\zeta\), and suppose
\[
|\Psi_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}|\le \zeta
\quad
(\mathfrak i\in[r]_0,\ \mathfrak j\in[c]_0).
\]
For the canonical vector \(e^{(c)}_{\mathfrak j}\in\mathbb R^c\),
\[
\|\Psi e^{(c)}_{\mathfrak j}\|_2
\le
\sum_{\mathfrak i\in[r]_0}|\Psi_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}|
\le r\,\zeta .
\]
Thus, for every \(\upsilon\in\mathbb R^c\) with \(\|\upsilon\|_2\le1\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|\Psi\upsilon\|_2
&=
\left\|
\sum_{\mathfrak j\in[c]_0}
(J_c\upsilon)_{\mathfrak j}\,\Psi e^{(c)}_{\mathfrak j}
\right\|_2  \\
&\le
\sum_{\mathfrak j\in[c]_0}
|(J_c\upsilon)_{\mathfrak j}|\,
\|\Psi e^{(c)}_{\mathfrak j}\|_2  \\
&\le
c\,r\,\|J_c\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\zeta .
\end{aligned}
\]
Using the ordinary matrix action and operator-norm convention in
\cref{obj:synth_30,obj:synth_35}, taking the supremum over the Euclidean
unit ball gives
\[
\|\Psi\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\le
\operatorname{Ent}_{r,c}\,\zeta,
\qquad
\operatorname{Ent}_{r,c}=c\,r\,\|J_c\|_{\mathrm{op}} .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.matrixNorm_le_entryBound

\item Apply the preceding estimate with
\[
r=d_z,\qquad c=d_x,\qquad \zeta=\varrho
\]
to the four matrix differences \(s_\ell-s'_\ell\), \(\ell=1,2,3,4\).  The
coordinate bounds from the first step yield
\[
\|s_\ell-s'_\ell\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\le
\operatorname{Ent}_{d_z,d_x}\,\varrho,
\qquad \ell=1,2,3,4 .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.dS_le_dist_mul

\item For the mean block, expand in the canonical basis of \(\mathbb R^{d_x}\):
\[
s_5-s'_5
=
\sum_{\mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0}
\bigl((s_5)_{\mathfrak j}-(s'_5)_{\mathfrak j}\bigr)e^{(d_x)}_{\mathfrak j}.
\]
The coordinate bounds and the triangle inequality give
\[
\|s_5-s'_5\|_2
\le
\sum_{\mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0}
\bigl|(s_5)_{\mathfrak j}-(s'_5)_{\mathfrak j}\bigr|
\le
d_x\,\varrho .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.eucNorm_le_card_mul_bound

\item Substituting the four matrix-block estimates and the mean-block estimate
into the definition of \(d_S\) in \cref{obj:def:observable-summary} gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
d_S(s,s')
&=
\sum_{\ell=1}^{4}\|s_\ell-s'_\ell\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+\|s_5-s'_5\|_2 \\
&\le
4\,\operatorname{Ent}_{d_z,d_x}\,\varrho+d_x\,\varrho \\
&=
\bigl(4\operatorname{Ent}_{d_z,d_x}+d_x\bigr)
\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}(s,s') .
\end{aligned}
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.dS_le_dist_mul
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:summary-metric-continuity}[Summary metric continuity]
For fixed target- and reference-proxy dimensions \(d_x,d_z\), let \(d_S\) be the summary metric on observable moment summaries from \cref{obj:def:observable-summary}. The map
\[
(s,q)\longmapsto d_S(s,q)
\]
from the product of the five-block summary space with itself to \(\mathbb R\) is continuous.
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:summary-metric-continuity}]
By \cref{obj:synth_27}, for the fixed dimensions \(d_x\) and \(d_z\) the ambient summary space is
\[
\mathscr S_{\mathrm{all}}
=
(\mathbb R^{d_z\times d_x})^4\times \mathbb R^{d_x}.
\]
Write
\[
s=\bigl(M_0(s),M_1(s),N_0(s),N_1(s),m_X(s)\bigr),
\qquad
q=\bigl(M_0(q),M_1(q),N_0(q),N_1(q),m_X(q)\bigr).
\]
The coordinate map
\[
\iota(s):=\bigl(M_0(s),M_1(s),N_0(s),N_1(s),m_X(s)\bigr)
\]
is continuous for the product topology, and therefore each coordinate projection
\[
s\mapsto M_0(s),\quad s\mapsto M_1(s),\quad
s\mapsto N_0(s),\quad s\mapsto N_1(s),\quad
s\mapsto m_X(s)
\]
is continuous. The same finite-product argument covers the zero-dimensional cases, where the relevant products and sums have their usual singleton or empty interpretation. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.dS_continuous

For a pair \((s,q)\), the four matrix-valued difference maps
\[
(s,q)\longmapsto M_0(s)-M_0(q),\quad
(s,q)\longmapsto M_1(s)-M_1(q),
\]
\[
(s,q)\longmapsto N_0(s)-N_0(q),\quad
(s,q)\longmapsto N_1(s)-N_1(q)
\]
are continuous by continuity of the coordinate projections and of subtraction. If \(\Upsilon\in\mathbb R^{d_z\times d_x}\), the associated Euclidean linear operator
\[
\mathbb R^{d_x}\to\mathbb R^{d_z},
\qquad x\longmapsto \Upsilon x,
\]
depends continuously on \(\Upsilon\), because this matrix-to-operator assignment is linear between finite-dimensional normed spaces. Composing with the continuous operator norm gives continuity of
\[
(s,q)\longmapsto \|M_0(s)-M_0(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}},\quad
(s,q)\longmapsto \|M_1(s)-M_1(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}},
\]
\[
(s,q)\longmapsto \|N_0(s)-N_0(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}},\quad
(s,q)\longmapsto \|N_1(s)-N_1(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.matrixCLM_continuous

The mean-coordinate difference
\[
(s,q)\longmapsto m_X(s)-m_X(q)
\]
is continuous as a map into \(\mathbb R^{d_x}\). Its Euclidean norm is
\[
\|m_X(s)-m_X(q)\|_2
=
\left(\sum_{i<d_x}\bigl(m_X(s)_i-m_X(q)_i\bigr)^2\right)^{1/2}.
\]
The summand coordinates are continuous, the index set is finite, and the square-root is continuous on the nonnegative range of the sum of squares. Hence
\[
(s,q)\longmapsto \|m_X(s)-m_X(q)\|_2
\]
is continuous. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.dS_continuous

Using \cref{obj:def:observable-summary},
\[
d_S(s,q)
=
\|M_0(s)-M_0(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+\|M_1(s)-M_1(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+\|N_0(s)-N_0(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+\|N_1(s)-N_1(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+\|m_X(s)-m_X(q)\|_2 .
\]
This is a finite sum of continuous real-valued functions on
\(\mathscr S_{\mathrm{all}}\times\mathscr S_{\mathrm{all}}\). Therefore
\[
(s,q)\longmapsto d_S(s,q)
\]
is continuous. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.ProxyEffectlawEigencollisionFrontier.dS_continuous
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:stacked-proxy-moment-signal-margin}[Stacked proxy signal margin]
Let \(P\), a full-data probability law carrying \((U,T,X,Z,Y(0),Y(1),Y)\), belong to the uniformly conditioned proxy causal model class \(\mathcal M\) in \cref{obj:def:model-class}. Suppose:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Dimensions.)} \(2\le k\le d_x\), where \(k\) is the number of latent classes and \(d_x\) is the target-proxy dimension.
\item \textbf{(Radius.)} \(L\ge 1\), with the bounded target-proxy condition in \cref{obj:ass:bounded-x}.
\item \textbf{(Margins.)} \(\pi_0>0\) and \(\sigma_0>0\), with the latent-arm positivity and proxy-rank margin conditions in \cref{obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity,obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin}.
\end{itemize}
Then the vertically stacked observable proxy moment satisfies
\[
\pi_0\sigma_0^2
\le
\sigma_{k-1}\!\left\{\operatorname{stack}\bigl(M_0(P),M_1(P)\bigr)\right\},
\]
where \(\sigma_{k-1}\) uses zero-based singular-value indexing and \(M_0(P),M_1(P)\) are the armwise \(ZX^\top\) population moments from \cref{obj:def:observable-population-moments}.
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:stacked-proxy-moment-signal-margin}]
Set
\[
A_0:=\bigl(E_P[Z_i\mid U=u,T=0]\bigr)_{i,u},\qquad
B:=\bigl(E_P[X_i\mid U=u]\bigr)_{i,u},
\]
and
\[
\Gamma_0:=
\operatorname{diag}\left\{
\frac{P(U=u,T=0)}{P(T=0)}:1\le u\le k
\right\}.
\]
Let \(S=\operatorname{range}(B)\subseteq\mathbb R^{d_x}\).  The proxy-rank margin in
\cref{obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin} gives
\[
\sigma_{k-1}(A_0)\ge \sigma_0,\qquad
\sigma_{k-1}(B)\ge \sigma_0 .
\]
Since \(\sigma_0>0\), the maps induced by \(A_0\) and \(B\) are injective.  Hence
\[
\dim S=k .
\]
% lean: stackedProxyMoment_minSingular

The latent-arm positivity condition gives \(P(U=u,T=0)\ge \pi_0\) for every \(u\).  Since
\(0<P(T=0)\le1\), each diagonal entry of \(\Gamma_0\) is at least \(\pi_0\).  Therefore, for every
\(y\in\mathbb R^k\),
\[
\pi_0\|y\|_2\le \|\Gamma_0y\|_2 .
\]
% lean: latentArmWeights_minSingular

Now fix \(x\in S\).  The least-singular-value bound for \(B\), restricted to its range, gives
\[
\sigma_0\|x\|_2\le \|B^\top x\|_2 .
\]
% lean: least_singularValue_mul_norm_le_adjoint_on_range
Applying the preceding diagonal bound to \(y=B^\top x\) gives
\[
\pi_0\|B^\top x\|_2\le \|\Gamma_0B^\top x\|_2 .
\]
% lean: latentArmWeights_minSingular
The least-singular-value bound for \(A_0\) gives
\[
\sigma_0\|\Gamma_0B^\top x\|_2
\le
\|A_0\Gamma_0B^\top x\|_2 .
\]
% lean: least_singularValue_mul_norm_le
Combining these three inequalities,
\[
\pi_0\sigma_0^2\|x\|_2
\le
\|A_0\Gamma_0B^\top x\|_2 .
\]
% lean: stackedProxyMoment_minSingular

By the observable proxy moment factorization in
\cref{obj:lem:observed-proxy-moment-factorization},
\[
M_0(P)=A_0\Gamma_0B^\top .
\]
Thus
\[
\pi_0\sigma_0^2\|x\|_2
\le
\|M_0(P)x\|_2 .
\]
% lean: observedProxyMoment_factorization
For
\[
G:=\operatorname{stack}\bigl(M_0(P),M_1(P)\bigr),
\]
vertical stacking gives the Pythagorean identity
\[
\|Gx\|_2^2=\|M_0(P)x\|_2^2+\|M_1(P)x\|_2^2,
\]
and hence
\[
\|M_0(P)x\|_2\le \|Gx\|_2 .
\]
% lean: observedProxyMoment_norm_le_stackedProxyMoment
Consequently,
\[
\pi_0\sigma_0^2\|x\|_2\le \|Gx\|_2
\qquad\text{for every }x\in S .
\]
% lean: stackedProxyMoment_minSingular

Since \(S\) is a \(k\)-dimensional subspace and \(\pi_0\sigma_0^2\ge0\), the variational
subspace characterization of zero-indexed singular values yields
\[
\pi_0\sigma_0^2
\le
\sigma_{k-1}(G)
=
\sigma_{k-1}\!\left\{\operatorname{stack}\bigl(M_0(P),M_1(P)\bigr)\right\}.
\]
% lean: le_singularValues_of_subspace
\end{proof}

These witness calculations locate the explicit two-class comparisons inside the same model restrictions used for the upper bounds. The path-local statement calibrates the observed Kullback--Leibler radius to the product \(g^2h^2\), which is the scale used by \cref{obj:thm:matching-local-lower-bounds}.

The final two auxiliary statements concern ordered masses as functions of represented atomic laws. They ensure that, when the support is separated, the ordered-weight target depends on the aggregate measure and is invariant across labelled coordinate representations.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:model-compressed-spectral-certificate}[Compressed spectral certificate]
Let \(k,d_x,d_z\in\mathbb N\), let \(L,\pi_0,\sigma_0\in\mathbb R\), and let \(Q\) be a full-data probability law in the uniformly conditioned model class \(\mathcal M\) of \cref{obj:def:model-class}, with observable summary \(S(Q)\) as in \cref{obj:def:observable-summary}. Assume that \(Q\) satisfies \cref{obj:ass:anchor,obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin,obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity}. Let \(M_t(S(Q))\) be the \(t\)-arm \(ZX^\top\) moment block from \cref{obj:def:observable-population-moments}, and set
\[
G:=\operatorname{stack}(M_0(S(Q)),M_1(S(Q))).
\]
Then there exist a signal basis \(V\) spanning the signal row space of \(S(Q)\) and a real diagonalization of the compressed effect operator \(\Delta Q(Q)\) from \cref{obj:def:compressed-operator} such that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for each arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\), the compressed map
\[
x\longmapsto M_t(S(Q))Vx
\]
is injective;
\item the stacked proxy moment has rank
\[
\operatorname{rank}(G)=k;
\]
\item for every index \(j\),
\[
\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}\le \sigma_j(G)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
j<k;
\]
\item the eigenvalue vector of the diagonalization is the latent-effect vector \((\tau_u(Q))_u\) from \cref{obj:def:latent-effect};
\item writing \(R\) for the diagonalizing basis matrix, the left and right spectral anchors satisfy
\[
a(Q)=R^{-\top}p(Q),
\qquad
R^{-1}c(Q)=\mathbf 1_k,
\]
where \(p(Q)=(p_u(Q))_u\) is the latent-mass vector from \cref{obj:def:latent-mass}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:model-compressed-spectral-certificate}]
\begin{enumerate}
\item
Write
\[
B:=B(Q)\in\mathbb R^{d_x\times k}
\]
for the target-proxy feature matrix from \cref{obj:def:target-feature}.  The parameter domain in \cref{obj:def:model-class} gives \(2\le k\), \(k\le d_x\), \(1\le L\), \(\pi_0>0\), and \(\sigma_0>0\).  With zero-based singular-value indexing, \cref{obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin} gives
\[
\sigma_{k-1}(B)\ge \sigma_0>0 .
\]
Hence the first \(k\) singular values of \(B\) are positive.  Choose the thin singular-value factorization
\[
B=V\Upsilon ,
\]
where \(V\in\mathbb R^{d_x\times k}\) has orthonormal columns and \(\Upsilon\in\mathbb R^{k\times k}\) is invertible.  Put
\[
\Theta:=B^\top V=\Upsilon^\top,
\qquad
\Theta^{-1}:=\Upsilon^{-T}.
\]
Then
\[
\Theta\Theta^{-1}=\Theta^{-1}\Theta=I_k .
\]
% lean: thinSignalFactorization
% lean: SignalBasis.transpose_mul_self

\item
For each arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\), define
\[
\Gamma_t
:=
\operatorname{diag}\left\{
\frac{Q(U=u,T=t)}{Q(T=t)}:1\le u\le k
\right\},
\qquad
\Xi_t:=A_t\Gamma_t ,
\]
where \(A_t=A_t(Q)\) is the reference-proxy feature matrix from \cref{obj:def:reference-feature}.  At the model-generated summary \(S(Q)\), \cref{obj:lem:observed-proxy-moment-factorization,obj:lem:observed-outcome-proxy-moment-factorization} give
\[
M_t(S(Q))=A_t\Gamma_tB^\top=\Xi_tB^\top
\]
and
\[
N_t(S(Q))
=
A_t\Gamma_t\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}B^\top
=
\Xi_t\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}B^\top .
\]
Multiplying by \(V\) gives
\[
M_t(S(Q))V=\Xi_t\Theta,
\qquad
N_t(S(Q))V
=
\Xi_t\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta .
\]

Also,
\[
M_t(S(Q))^\top
=
B\Xi_t^\top
=
V\Upsilon\Xi_t^\top ,
\]
so the row space of each \(M_t(S(Q))\) is contained in \(\operatorname{range}(V)\).  For \(t=0\), latent-arm positivity in \cref{obj:ass:latent-arm-positivity} gives
\[
\Gamma_0(u,u)=\frac{Q(U=u,T=0)}{Q(T=0)}\ge \pi_0
\]
for every \(u\), since \(Q(U=u,T=0)\ge\pi_0\) and \(Q(T=0)\le1\).  Combining this with \cref{obj:ass:proxy-rank-margin} and the standard product lower bound for the least signal singular value,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_0(S(Q))\right)
&=
\sigma_{k-1}\!\left(A_0\Gamma_0B^\top\right)  \\
&\ge
\sigma_{k-1}(A_0)\,\sigma_{k-1}(\Gamma_0)\,\sigma_{k-1}(B)
\ge
\pi_0\sigma_0^2 .
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus \(M_0(S(Q))\) has row rank \(k\).  Its row space is contained in the \(k\)-dimensional space \(\operatorname{range}(V)\), hence
\[
\operatorname{range}\!\left(M_0(S(Q))^\top\right)=\operatorname{range}(V).
\]
Since the row space of \(M_1(S(Q))\) is contained in the same space, the signal row space of \(S(Q)\) equals
\[
\operatorname{range}\!\left(M_0(S(Q))^\top\right)
+
\operatorname{range}\!\left(M_1(S(Q))^\top\right)
=
\operatorname{range}(V).
\]
Thus \(V\) spans the signal row space of \(S(Q)\).
% lean: observedProxyMoment_factorization
% lean: observedOutcomeProxyMoment_factorization
% lean: observedProxyMoment_minSingular_of_factorization
% lean: modelCompressedSpectralFacts_exists

\item
The same product-margin argument for each arm \(t\) gives
\[
\pi_0\sigma_0^2
\le
\sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_t(S(Q))\right).
\]
Because \(V\) is an orthonormal basis for the signal row space, compression by \(V\) preserves the \(k\) nonzero signal singular values:
\[
\sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_t(S(Q))V\right)
=
\sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_t(S(Q))\right).
\]
Consequently
\[
0<\pi_0\sigma_0^2
\le
\sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_t(S(Q))V\right),
\]
and the linear map
\[
x\longmapsto M_t(S(Q))Vx,\qquad x\in\mathbb R^k,
\]
is injective for each \(t\in\{0,1\}\).
% lean: observedProxyMoment_compression_margin
% lean: publishedMomentIdentity_injective_of_signalMinSingular_pos

\item
Let
\[
G:=\operatorname{stack}\bigl(M_0(S(Q)),M_1(S(Q))\bigr).
\]
By \cref{obj:lem:stacked-proxy-moment-signal-margin},
\[
\pi_0\sigma_0^2\le \sigma_{k-1}(G),
\]
so \(\operatorname{rank}(G)\ge k\).  On the other hand, if
\[
\Xi_{\mathrm{st}}:=\operatorname{stack}(\Xi_0,\Xi_1)\in\mathbb R^{2d_z\times k},
\]
then the proxy factorizations give
\[
G=\Xi_{\mathrm{st}}B^\top .
\]
Hence \(\operatorname{rank}(G)\le k\), and therefore
\[
\operatorname{rank}(G)=k .
\]
The threshold characterization follows immediately.  If \(j<k\), monotonicity of singular values gives
\[
\sigma_j(G)\ge\sigma_{k-1}(G)\ge\pi_0\sigma_0^2
\ge\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}.
\]
If \(k\le j\), the rank identity gives \(\sigma_j(G)=0\), while
\[
0<\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}.
\]
Thus, for every singular-value index \(j\),
\[
\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}\le \sigma_j(G)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
j<k .
\]
% lean: stackedProxyMoment_minSingular
% lean: modelCompressedSpectralFacts_exists

\item
Since \(M_t(S(Q))V\) is injective, its Moore--Penrose inverse is a left inverse on its range:
\[
\{M_t(S(Q))V\}^{\dagger}M_t(S(Q))V=I_k .
\]
Using the displayed factorizations,
\[
N_t(S(Q))V
=
\{M_t(S(Q))V\}\,
\Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta .
\]
Therefore
\[
\{M_t(S(Q))V\}^{\dagger}N_t(S(Q))V
=
\Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta .
\]
By the compressed-operator formula in \cref{obj:def:compressed-operator},
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Delta Q(Q)
&=
\Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{1u}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta
-
\Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{0u}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta  \\
&=
